h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Plug-and-Play ADMM:
#   x-step : prox of data term 0.5||Op x - y||^2  (solved with inner CG)
#   z-step : "prox" of the prior replaced by a 3x3 median filter (denoiser)
# The median filter has no strength parameter, so the regularisation weight is
# controlled by the ADMM penalty tau (larger tau -> data term weighs more).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
l2 = pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y, niter=30, warm=True)


def denoiser(x, tau):
    return median_filter(x, size=3, mode="nearest")


x0 = np.zeros(ny * nx)
tau = 3.0     # chosen by a small sweep (0.1-10); 3-5 is the sweet spot
niter = 200   # SNR stable to within ~0.03 dB from 200 to 1000 iterations
xinv = pyproximal.optimization.pnp.PlugAndPlay(
    l2, denoiser, (ny, nx), solver=pyproximal.optimization.primal.ADMM,
    tau=tau, x0=x0, niter=niter, show=False,
)[0]
xinv = xinv.reshape(ny, nx)

# baselines for comparison
xls = pylops.optimization.basic.cgls(Op, y, niter=30)[0].reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"SNR blurred data    : {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, y.reshape(ny, nx)):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR CGLS (30 it)    : {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR CGLS + median   : {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, median_filter(xls, 3)):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR PnP-ADMM median : {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The fix is to switch from plain `lsqr` on `Op` to regularised least squares with a `SecondDerivative` operator. I ran it on your example:

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
sigma = 0.05
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Smoothing regulariser: second derivative of the model
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

def solve(eps):
    # minimises ||y - Op x||^2 + eps^2 ||D2 x||^2  (solved with LSQR internally)
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[0]

# Pick eps with the discrepancy principle: make the residual match the noise level
target = np.sqrt(n) * sigma
lo, hi = -2.0, 3.0                      # search range in log10(eps)
for _ in range(30):
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    r = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ solve(10 ** mid))
    lo, hi = (mid, hi) if r < target else (lo, mid)
eps = 10 ** mid
xinv = solve(eps)
```

**Why the plain version is noisy:** the Gaussian kernel almost wipes out high frequencies. Without a regulariser, LSQR runs 300 iterations and puts amplified noise into those frequencies. Your current result has a relative error of about **1600%** (`‖x−xtrue‖/‖xtrue‖ ≈ 16`).

**Choosing the weight.** The residual grows steadily as `eps` increases, so you can choose `eps` so that the residual equals the expected noise norm, `√n·σ ≈ 0.87`. This is the discrepancy principle. For your data it picks **eps ≈ 18**, and the relative error drops to **about 5%**.

Results from a sweep on your example:

| eps | residual | rel. error |
|---|---|---|
| 0.01 | 0.75 | 119% |
| 0.32 | 0.76 | 11% |
| 3.2 | 0.78 | **2.3%** |
| 18 (discrepancy) | 0.87 | 5.1% |
| 100 | 3.1 | 30% |

Some notes:
- **Oversmoothing:** the discrepancy principle tends to smooth a little too much. Here the best possible `eps`, which you can only find because the true model is known, is around 3–10. Still, it's a principled choice that doesn't need the true model, and it lands close.
- **If you don't know σ:** you can't compute the target, so use an L-curve instead. Sweep `eps` on a log grid, plot `log‖y−Op x‖` against `log‖D2 x‖`, and take the corner. Generalised cross-validation (GCV) is another option.
- **Scale of `eps`:** `SecondDerivative` uses `sampling=1` by default, so the `eps` values above depend on that. If you pass `sampling=dx`, the operator is scaled by 1/dx², and the best `eps` shifts to match.
- **`edge=True`** uses one-sided stencils at the ends, so the boundary samples are regularised too.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The fix is to add a second-derivative operator as a regularisation term. The problem stays ordinary least squares, so you don't need PyProximal. A good way to pick the weight is the discrepancy principle: you know the noise level (σ = 0.05), so choose the weight that makes the data residual about the same size as the noise, ‖y − Op x‖ ≈ σ√n.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
sigma = 0.05
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Second-derivative regulariser
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

def solve(eps):
    # minimises ||y - Op x||^2 + eps^2 ||D2 x||^2
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=1000)[0]

# Discrepancy principle: pick eps so that ||y - Op x|| ≈ sigma * sqrt(n)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
lo, hi = -3.0, 3.0                      # search log10(eps) in [1e-3, 1e3]
for _ in range(25):                     # bisection; residual grows with eps
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    r = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ solve(10**mid))
    lo, hi = (mid, hi) if r < target else (lo, mid)
eps = 10**mid
xinv = solve(eps)
print(f"eps = {eps:.3g}")
```

**Results from running this with pylops 2.8.0:**

| Method | Relative error ‖x − x_true‖/‖x_true‖ |
|---|---|
| Your plain `lsqr` (300 iters) | **16.0** (dominated by noise) |
| D2 regularisation, eps from a sweep 0.01 → 100 | 1.19 → **0.024 (best, eps≈3)** → 0.30 |
| D2 regularisation, discrepancy-principle eps ≈ 18 | **0.051** |

**Notes:**
- **How the weight works:** `epsRs` multiplies the operator, so the penalty is eps²‖D2 x‖². Doubling eps makes the smoothing four times stronger.
- **Why the discrepancy principle:** in this test it picked a weight a bit larger than the best one. That's normal, because it tends to over-smooth slightly. It's still a sound choice when you know σ and don't know the true model, which is the real-world case.
- **If you don't know σ:** sweep eps on a log scale and plot ‖y − Op x‖ against ‖D2 x‖ on log-log axes (an L-curve). Pick the eps at the corner.
- **Iterations:** more iterations help now. Without regularisation, running more iterations kept fitting noise (semi-convergence). With the regulariser the problem is well-posed, so the iteration limit only needs to be high enough to converge.

Use `pylops.SecondDerivative` as the regularisation operator and solve with `regularized_inversion`. Pick the weight with the **discrepancy principle**: choose `eps` so the data residual matches the noise level, ‖y − Op x‖ ≈ σ√n. Here σ = 0.05, so the target is ≈ 0.87.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops
from scipy.optimize import brentq

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
sigma = 0.05
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Second-derivative smoothing operator
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

def solve(eps):
    # minimises ||y - Op x||^2 + eps^2 ||D2 x||^2
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500)[0]

# Discrepancy principle: find eps so the residual equals the expected noise norm
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
eps = brentq(lambda e: np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ solve(e)) - target, 1e-1, 1e3, xtol=0.1)
xinv = solve(eps)
```

**Results from running this**

| | relative error ‖x − xtrue‖/‖xtrue‖ |
|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr`, 300 iterations | **16.0** (1600%, dominated by noise) |
| D2-regularised, eps ≈ 18.4 (discrepancy principle) | **0.051** |

Sweep of `eps` values for comparison:

| eps | residual | rel. error |
|---|---|---|
| 0.1 | 0.76 | 0.205 |
| 1 | 0.77 | 0.058 |
| 4.6 | 0.78 | 0.020 |
| 10 | 0.80 | 0.026 |
| **18.4** | **0.87** | **0.051** |
| 46 | 1.66 | 0.159 |
| 100+ | ≥3.1 | ≥0.30 |

**Notes**
- **`eps` is squared in the objective.** `epsRs` scales the operator inside the stacked system, so the penalty is eps²‖D2 x‖². Keep that in mind if you compare this weight with one from another tool.
- **You don't need to tune it exactly.** Anything from about 2 to 20 works well here. The discrepancy principle slightly over-smooths: the best error in the sweep was at eps ≈ 5. That's still the right default, because it only needs the noise level and not the true model.
- **If you don't know σ,** use an L-curve (plot log‖y − Op x‖ against log‖D2 x‖ over the sweep and take the corner) or generalised cross-validation (GCV) instead.
- **`edge=True`** uses one-sided stencils at the boundaries so the ends of the model are also constrained.
- **Stopping `lsqr` early** is the other way to get some regularisation. Your 300 iterations let it fit the noise, and that's why plain `lsqr` blew up. The explicit D2 term gives you a weight you can control, so I'd use that.

Swap plain `lsqr` for `regularized_inversion`, using a `SecondDerivative` operator as the regulariser. That solves:

  min ‖y − Op x‖² + ε² ‖D₂ x‖²

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

eps = 15.0
xinv = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps],
    iter_lim=500, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10,   # passed on to scipy's lsqr
)[0]
```

**Choosing ε.** You know the noise level (σ = 0.05), so you can use the discrepancy principle. It says to pick the largest ε whose data residual ‖y − Op x‖ is still about the size of the noise, σ√n ≈ 0.87. I ran your script with a range of ε values:

| ε | ‖y − Op x‖ | relative error vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|---|
| none (plain lsqr) | – | **16.0** (the noise blows up) |
| 1 | 0.77 | 0.058 |
| 4.6 | 0.78 | **0.020** (best possible) |
| 10 | 0.80 | 0.026 |
| ~18 | ≈ 0.87 ← discrepancy target | ~0.05 |
| 46 | 1.66 | 0.16 |
| 100+ | 3.1+ | 0.30+ (over-smoothed) |

- **Without regularisation** the error is about 1600%. The Gaussian blur wipes out high frequencies, so lsqr fits the noise.
- **Anywhere from ε ≈ 2 to 20** you get a good result, with 2–6% error. The discrepancy principle points to ε ≈ 15–20, which leans slightly towards over-smoothing. That's normal for this method, and it's safe.
- **Above ε ≈ 30** the residual grows well past the noise level, meaning you're smoothing away real signal.

To choose ε automatically:

```python
sigma = 0.05
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
for eps in np.logspace(-1, 3, 25):
    x = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[0]
    if np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x) > target:
        break
    xinv = x   # keep the last eps whose residual is still below the noise level
```

If you don't know σ, use an L-curve instead. Plot ‖y − Op x‖ against ‖D₂x‖ on log-log axes and pick ε at the corner. Here the corner falls around ε ≈ 5–20, which agrees with the table.

**Two more points:**
- `edge=True` makes the operator use one-sided differences at the boundaries, so the ends of the model are regularised too.
- Use more iterations with tight tolerances, like the settings above. Stopping plain lsqr early also acts as a regulariser, and you don't want that mixed in with the one you're controlling through ε.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Your plain `lsqr` has no regulariser, so after 300 iterations it fits the noise and blows up (relative error ≈ 16, i.e. 1600%). The fix is to add a second-derivative operator `D2` and minimise

‖y − Op x‖² + ε² ‖D2 x‖²

PyLops has a solver for exactly this, `regularized_inversion`. To choose ε, use the **discrepancy principle**: you know the noise level (σ = 0.05), so pick the ε where the data residual ‖Op x − y‖ is about σ√n. That means you fit the data only as closely as the noise allows.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
sigma = 0.05
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Smoothing regulariser: second derivative
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True, dtype="float64")

def solve(eps):
    return regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500)[0]

# Discrepancy principle: find the eps where ||Op x - y|| ≈ sigma * sqrt(n).
# The residual grows with eps, so bisect on log10(eps).
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
lo, hi = -2.0, 3.0
for _ in range(30):
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    r = np.linalg.norm(Op @ solve(10**mid) - y)
    lo, hi = (mid, hi) if r < target else (lo, mid)
eps = 10**mid
xinv = solve(eps)
print(f"eps = {eps:.2f}, rel. error = {np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue)/np.linalg.norm(xtrue):.3f}")
```

I ran a sweep of ε on your exact setup:

| ε | ‖Op x − y‖ | relative error |
|---|---|---|
| no regularisation | – | 16.0 |
| 0.1 | 0.760 | 0.205 |
| 1 | 0.770 | 0.058 |
| 5.6 | 0.786 | **0.020** (best possible) |
| **≈18 (discrepancy)** | **0.858 ≈ target 0.866** | **0.049** |
| 100 | 3.108 | 0.299 |

The discrepancy principle picks ε ≈ 18 and gives about 5% error, down from 1600%. The best ε in hindsight (~5) was a bit smaller. The discrepancy principle tends to choose slightly too much smoothing, which is usually what you want.

Some notes:
- **ε depends on scaling.** `SecondDerivative` uses `sampling=1` by default. If you pass `sampling=dx`, the operator gets 1/dx² times bigger and the right ε gets that much smaller. Always choose ε with a criterion like this one; don't carry a number over from another setup.
- **If you don't know σ**, use an L-curve instead. Plot log‖D2 x‖ against log‖Op x − y‖ over a log-spaced range of ε and pick the corner. Generalised cross-validation (GCV) is another option.
- **`edge=True`** makes the derivative well-behaved at the ends of the signal. Without it the end samples are less constrained.
- **Regularisation is what fixes the noise here, not `niter`.** You can also stop plain `lsqr` early (a few tens of iterations) as a crude smoother, but ε is much easier to control and justify.

The noise comes from the operator: a Gaussian blur with σ = 6 samples wipes out high frequencies, so plain least squares blows up the noise to fit the data. Use `regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` operator, and choose the weight with the **discrepancy principle**: pick the weight where the data misfit `‖Op x − y‖` equals the expected noise norm, `σ√n`. You know σ = 0.05, so this works well here.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops
from scipy.optimize import brentq

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
sigma = 0.05
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Smoothing regulariser: second derivative of the model
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

def solve(eps):
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10
    )[0]

# Discrepancy principle: find eps where the misfit equals the noise norm
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
f = lambda logeps: np.linalg.norm(Op @ solve(10**logeps) - y) - target
eps = 10 ** brentq(f, -1, 3, xtol=1e-3)
xinv = solve(eps)
```

This minimises `‖y − Op x‖² + ε²‖D₂x‖²`. Note that `epsRs` multiplies the operator, so the penalty scales with ε².

**Results from running it (pylops 2.8.0), as relative model error `‖x − xtrue‖/‖xtrue‖`:**

| Method | ε | Misfit | Error |
|---|---|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr`, 300 iterations | none | none | **16.0** (1600%) |
| Weight sweep | 0.1 | 0.76 | 0.20 |
| | 4.6 | 0.78 | 0.020 (best, but needs `xtrue` to find) |
| | 21.5 | 0.92 | 0.062 |
| | 100 | 3.1 | 0.30 (too smooth) |
| **Discrepancy principle** | **≈18.4** | **0.866 (= σ√n)** | **0.051** |

The discrepancy principle smooths a little more than the hindsight-best value, which is typical, but it only needs the noise level and not the true model.

**If you don't know σ:**
- **L-curve:** plot `log‖Op x − y‖` against `log‖D2 x‖` over a log-spaced range of ε (as in the sweep above) and pick the corner.
- **Estimate σ from the data:** for example, use the high-frequency part of the spectrum of `y`, where the blurred signal has almost no energy.

**The weight depends on scaling.** `SecondDerivative` uses `sampling=1` by default. If you change `sampling` or rescale the data, the right ε changes too, so re-run the search instead of reusing 18.

Prompt

My deconvolution with lsqr gives a very noisy model. I'd like to add a smoothing regulariser on the second derivative of the model and pick a sensible weight for it. How should I change this?

import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=300)[0]
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: this is a plain Tikhonov (quadratic) regularisation problem. The natural PyLops change is pylops.SecondDerivative(n) as a regulariser in pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], ...) (or an equivalent augmented system VStack([Op, eps*D2]) with [y, 0] solved by lsqr/cgls, or the normal equations), with the weight chosen by a principled procedure: an L-curve, the discrepancy principle (residual ≈ noise level 0.05·√n), GCV, or a sweep scored against the known synthetic xtrue.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It adds a second-derivative (not first-derivative only, not identity only) quadratic regulariser to the least-squares problem with PyLops operators, in code.
  • It gives a concrete way to choose the weight (one of the procedures above, or an equivalent one), not just a single guessed number.
  • It does not replace the problem with a non-smooth one (TV/L1/constraints) as the main recommendation.
  • Its main recommendation solves the quadratic problem with a least-squares solver (regularized_inversion, lsqr/cgls on the augmented system, or the normal equations), NOT with a proximal splitting / first-order proximal solver (ADMM, LinearizedADMM, PrimalDual, ProximalGradient, PyProximal in general): those are unnecessary for a purely quadratic objective. Mentioning them only as an aside is fine.

Otherwise FAIL.

The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the two signs are swapped, so it returns the negative of the true adjoint. With `<Ax, y>` and `<x, Aᴴy>` having opposite signs, `dottest` fails.

**Why:** your forward operator puts `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the superdiagonal:

```
A[i, i]   = -1
A[i, i+1] = +1
```

The adjoint is `x[j] = Σ_i A[i, j] · y[i]`, which works out to:

```
x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]      (y[-1] and y[n-1] are taken as 0)
```

- `y[j-1]` enters with `+`, so it's `x[1:] += y`
- `y[j]` enters with `-`, so it's `x[:-1] -= y`

Your code does the reverse and computes `y[j] - y[j-1]`.

**Fix:**

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:]  += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

With this change `dottest(Op, 99, 100)` should pass. The call itself was already correct: `nr=99, nc=100` matches `shape=(n-1, n)`. I didn't run it here.

**Tip:** on a small `n`, you can build the dense matrix and compare it with its transpose to see this kind of sign mistake directly:

```python
Op = ForwardDiff(5)
print(Op.todense())           # forward matrix
print(Op.H.todense())         # should equal Op.todense().T
```

The signs in your `_rmatvec` are flipped. It computes −Dᵀy instead of Dᵀy. Your `_matvec` is correct.

**Why:** the forward operator D has −1 on the diagonal and +1 on the superdiagonal:

```
y[i] = -x[i] + x[i+1]
```

When you transpose it, each `y[i]` goes back into the two entries it came from, with the same coefficients:

- `x[i]` gets `-y[i]`, so `x[:-1] -= y`
- `x[i+1]` gets `+y[i]`, so `x[1:] += y`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`).

You can spot this in the dottest output. The two inner products have the same magnitude and opposite signs:

```
Dot test failed, v^H(Opu)=-2.7858613122354816 - u^H(Op^Hv)=2.7858613122354843
```

When you see that pattern, the adjoint is off by a factor of −1.

**Fix:**

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

I ran both versions. The original fails as shown above, and the corrected version passes:

```
Dot test passed, v^H(Opu)=-3.5169043888937663 - u^H(Op^Hv)=-3.516904388893769
```

If you don't need a custom class, `pylops.FirstDerivative(100, kind="forward", edge=False)` gives a forward difference with a tested adjoint. Its output is shape `n` with the last sample set to zero, not `n-1`, so it's not an exact drop-in replacement.

The signs in your `_rmatvec` are backwards. Your adjoint computes **−Aᵀ**, not **Aᵀ**. The dot test output points to this: the two inner products have the same size and opposite signs (`-1.745…` vs `+1.745…`), which is what you get when the adjoint is exactly the negative of the correct one.

**Why:** the forward operator has `A[i, i+1] = +1` and `A[i, i] = -1`. The adjoint is `x[j] = Σᵢ A[i, j]·y[i]`, so:
- every `y[i]` is added to `x[i+1]` with a **+** sign, so `x[1:] += y`
- every `y[i]` is added to `x[i]` with a **−** sign, so `x[:-1] -= y`

**Fix:**

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the signs are swapped, so it returns the negative of the adjoint, −Aᵀy, instead of Aᵀy.

**Derivation.** The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. The adjoint has to satisfy ⟨Ax, y⟩ = ⟨x, Aᵀy⟩:

```
⟨Ax, y⟩ = Σᵢ (x[i+1] − x[i]) y[i]
        = Σⱼ x[j] (y[j−1] − y[j])     (y[−1] = y[n−1] = 0)
```

So the correct adjoint is `(Aᵀy)[j] = y[j−1] − y[j]`. That means each `y[i]` should be **added** to `x[i+1]` and **subtracted** from `x[i]`.

Your code does the opposite:

```python
x[1:]  -= y   # x[j] gets -y[j-1]   (should be +)
x[:-1] += y   # x[j] gets +y[j]     (should be -)
```

**Fix:**

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:]  += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

`dottest` caught this because ⟨Ax, y⟩ and ⟨x, Aᵀy⟩ come out with equal size but opposite sign. With `verb=True`, the two printed numbers should match except for the minus sign. That pattern almost always means the adjoint has a flipped sign.

If you just need the operator rather than writing your own, PyLops has one built in: `pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=False)`. Its output length is `n` rather than `n-1`, though, so its shape and boundary handling are different from your class.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped.** Your adjoint comes out as the negative of the true one.

## Why

The forward operator is the (n−1)×n matrix with **−1 on the main diagonal and +1 on the superdiagonal**:

```
y[i] = -1·x[i] + 1·x[i+1]   →   A[i, i] = -1,  A[i, i+1] = +1
```

The adjoint is the transpose, so each `y[i]` goes back to the same positions with the same weights:

```
x[i+1] += +1·y[i]   →   x[1:]  += y
x[i]   += -1·y[i]   →   x[:-1] -= y
```

Your code does `x[1:] -= y` and `x[:-1] += y`, which gives `-Aᵀy`. In the dot test you'll see `yᵀ(Ax)` and `xᵀ(Aᴴy)` with the same size but opposite signs.

## Fix

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:]  += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Equivalently, `return np.concatenate(([-y[0]], y[:-1] - y[1:], [y[-1]]))`, which is the negative backward difference with boundary terms.

Your `dottest(Op, 99, 100, ...)` call is fine because the arguments `nr=n-1` and `nc=n` match the operator's shape. After the fix it should pass.

**A quick way to catch this kind of bug:** for small `n`, build the dense matrices `Op.todense()` and `Op.H.todense()` and check `np.allclose(A.T, AH)`. A sign or index error shows up right away.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**The bug: your adjoint has the signs flipped.** `_rmatvec` returns `-Aᴴy` instead of `Aᴴy`. The shapes and the `dottest(Op, 99, 100)` call are both fine.

### Deriving the adjoint

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. The matrix `A` (size `(n-1) × n`) therefore has:
- `A[i, i]   = -1`
- `A[i, i+1] = +1`

The adjoint is `x = Aᵀy`, so `x[j] = Σᵢ A[i, j] · y[i]`:
- The `+1` entries at `(i, i+1)` give `+y[j-1]` to `x[j]` for `j = 1..n-1`, which is `x[1:] += y`.
- The `-1` entries at `(i, i)` give `-y[j]` to `x[j]` for `j = 0..n-2`, which is `x[:-1] -= y`.

Your code does the reverse (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`).

### Fix

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:]  += y   # from A[i, i+1] = +1
    x[:-1] -= y   # from A[i, i]   = -1
    return x
```

### How to recognise this in the `dottest` output

With `verb=True`, look at the two numbers it prints, `uᵀ(Av)` and `(Aᴴu)ᵀv`. With your current code they will have the **same magnitude but opposite signs**. That pattern almost always means a sign error in the adjoint. A mismatch in magnitude usually points to an indexing or offset error, or a missing scale factor.

If you want a second check, compare against PyLops' built-in first derivative:

```python
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(100, kind="forward", edge=False)
```

It computes the same stencil, but you'd need to account for its sampling scaling and its output shape (it returns length `n`, with the last sample zeroed), so it's a sanity check rather than an exact drop-in. Also note that `np.zeros(..., dtype=self.dtype)` is fine here. For complex dtypes this operator is real-valued, so no conjugation is needed in the adjoint.

Prompt

I wrote a forward-difference operator as a custom PyLops LinearOperator, but dottest fails. What's wrong?

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest

class ForwardDiff(pylops.LinearOperator):
    """y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i], i = 0..n-2"""
    def __init__(self, n, dtype="float64"):
        super().__init__(dtype=np.dtype(dtype), shape=(n - 1, n))
        self.n = n

    def _matvec(self, x):
        return x[1:] - x[:-1]

    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] -= y
        x[:-1] += y
        return x

Op = ForwardDiff(100)
dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is y[i] = x[i+1] − x[i], so its adjoint is x[:-1] −= y and x[1:] += y. The _rmatvec has the two slices swapped (x[1:] −= y; x[:-1] += y), which is the NEGATIVE of the adjoint: the dot-test gives ⟨Op x, y⟩ = −⟨x, Op^H y⟩ (same magnitude, opposite sign).

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It identifies that _rmatvec has the wrong sign / the slices swapped (the adjoint returned is minus the true one).
  • It gives a corrected _rmatvec equivalent to x[:-1] -= y; x[1:] += y (or x[:-1] = -y; x[1:] += y, or recommends pylops.FirstDerivative(..., kind="forward") with an equivalent correct definition).
  • It does not name dtype, shape or the dottest arguments as the cause.

Otherwise FAIL.
